1	# Observations: LR Standard explanations
2	
3	Survey date: 2026-09-26. Read-only. Primary corpus: the DB dump in `db/` (PT101 to PT159). The local markdown in `lsatu-wt-parser/Explanations/` (PT139 to PT159) was used to check layout and choice tags, which the dump does not include.

5	## 1. What the corpus actually is
6	
7	**Styles.** `lr_explanations.style` has four values: `single` 3,183, `standard` 236, `brief` 236, `another_way` 236. Every question with a `standard` row also has `brief` and `another_way`, and no question has both `standard` and `single`. So "the Standard explanation" is `standard` where it exists and `single` everywhere else. Both have the same shape (Argument breakdown steps, then five choice notes). 352 of 3,771 questions have no explanation at all (`is_explained = false`).
8

37	- The local `Explanations/PT140-explanations.md` is a thin, different version: about 100 words per question against about 630 in the DB. All 69 PT140 overlaps differ; every other PT139-159 question matches the DB within 20 words. Anyone reading PT140 from the markdown sees the wrong text.
38	- Bank alignment: 110 explained DB questions have no matching bank entry at the same PT/section/question, and 27 match a bank entry filed under another number (for example, the local PT148-S4-Q24 to Q26 stimuli sit in the bank under PT149-S4-Q24 to Q26). 40	- Leftover source references: "try this one" pointing at a missing link (PT139-S1-Q5), "Welcome to Test 89" (PT155-S4-Q15).

43	## 2. Quality by test range
44	
45	| PTs | Explained | Avg words | Complete | Em-dashes per 1k words | Notes |
46	|---|---|---|---|---|---|
47	| 101-122 | 1,158 | 553 | 85% | 0.5 | Full LSAT Lab voice. Many golden picks come from here. |
48	| 123-138 | 1,025 | 362 | 51% | 0.7 | Noticeably thinner. Most "incomplete" rows have a breakdown but no Goal step (346), and choice notes are often one or two lines. Some notes read like generic summaries (PT126-S4-Q20: "This introduces arbitrators, which are outside the scope"). |
49	| 139-156 | 1,097 | 588 | 91% | 0.6 | Same voice as 101-122. Consistently strong. |
50	| 157-159 | 139 | 672 | 89% | 7.1 | PT157/158 are explicit Standard. Breakdowns are in the house voice; choice notes are a different register (below). |
51	
52	**The PT157/158 explicit-Standard register.** Choice notes run about 100+ words each, open with formulas ("This answer captures...", "This answer identifies the argument's central flaw"), use em-dashes at roughly ten times the rate of everything else, and repeat the same point several ways. More importantly, they contain accuracy slips I did not find in the single-style text at the same rate:
53	- PT158-S3-Q2: the D note describes a different answer ("can effectively combine knowledge") from the actual choice ("can effectively solve many problems"); A is tagged "No Impact" while its note argues it undermines the argument.
54	- PT158-S2-Q16: the correct-answer note imports outside history (Plato's family wealth, Philip of Macedon) to justify the answer.
55	- PT158-S2-Q18: the A note argues against a claim the choice does not make.
56	
57	That is why no explicit-Standard item made the golden set, and why the house voice rules (no em-dashes) matter for the writing guide.
58	
59	**Two voices inside the single style.** Most text is one conversational author (voice A: "Hey, author", "Let's look for", FABS, Curious Fact, Given that / How can it be that). A second, more formal author appears in about 15% of questions (voice B: "Beware of answers that...", "If you chose this answer...", "This answer choice..."). Voice B is accurate and structured but lighter on prediction and analogy. Examples: PT146-S2-Q8, PT151-S2-Q1, PT152-S4-Q5, PT154-S1-Q10, PT155-S4-Q1. Both voices can produce a strong explanation; voice A produces most of the best ones.
60	
61	## 3. What separates strong from loose (the empirical comparison)
62	
63	I picked ten explanations that were clearly strong and ten that were complete but loose, read all twenty side by side, then measured a few things.
64	
65	- **Strong (10):** PT139-S4-Q18 Flaw, PT142-S4-Q22 Flaw, PT145-S2-Q19 Paradox, PT154-S1-Q18 NA, PT153-S2-Q13 Weaken, PT146-S2-Q13 Strengthen, PT147-S1-Q17 Evaluate, PT152-S2-Q21 Principle-Conform, PT150-S3-Q15 Agree/Disagree, PT144-S3-Q17 Role.
66	- **Loose but complete (10), with the failure each shows:** PT127-S2-Q3 Weaken (two wrong answers get one line each: "This supports the argument's explanation"); PT126-S4-Q20 Weaken (generic summary notes, a Goal that restates the task); PT124-S3-Q10 Role (short, and "Haha. This answer is unthinkably weird"); PT151-S2-Q18 Strengthen (117 words total; the 29% trap gets one sentence); PT146-S2-Q8 Flaw (every wrong answer is "The argument never discusses X", correct note is one line); PT142-S4-Q5 Principle (mechanical, no reason why the trap tempts); PT155-S2-Q10 Paradox (fine idea, thin notes); PT158-S3-Q2 Strengthen (long, but misquotes a choice); PT158-S2-Q16 Paradox (long, but brings in outside facts); PT148-S1-Q20 Method (sound analysis, but calls the correct answer "so, so dumb").
67	
68	Measured averages (strong vs loose):
69	
70	| Measure | Strong | Loose |
71	|---|---|---|
72	| Total words | 773 | 394 |
73	| Words in the Evaluate step | 169 | 77 |
74	| Words in the Goal step | 66 | 43 |
75	| Goal names 3+ content words from the stimulus | 0.9 | 0.5 |
76	| Avg words per wrong-answer note | 87 | 45 |
77	| Wrong-answer notes under 20 words (verdict only) | 0.0 per question | 1.1 per question |
78	| Wrong-answer notes that run an explicit test (negate it, does it weaken, does it trigger the rule, why should we believe it) | 1.4 of 4 | 0.2 of 4 |
79	| Words in the correct-answer note | 143 | 60 |
80	| Examples, analogies or worked numbers | 2.0 | 0.5 |
81	| Stated generalizations ("whenever", "on X questions", "usually") | 1.6 | 0.3 |
82	
83	What actually separates them, in order of how reliably it predicted my judgment:
84	
85	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument.** Strong ones do real work before the choices: they build the objection ("Given that X, how could we argue not-Y?"), do the arithmetic (25% of $400 vs 13% of $1,000), or set the two populations side by side. Loose ones restate the stimulus and move on.
86	2. **The Goal names the answer, not the task.** Strong: "look for an answer saying the fish in the leafless trees are exposed to predators that hunt by sight." Loose: "Find the flaw that mistakes a response to danger for a cause", "Identify an answer choice that supports fairness." Strong Goals often give two acceptable shapes (a linking answer or a defender).
87	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason tied to this question, and usually a test.** The loose group's signature is the verdict-only note: "This is irrelevant.", "That is unnecessary.", "The argument never discusses profits." Strong notes say what the answer claims, what was needed, and where they diverge, often by running the family's test on it (negate it; would it weaken; is the trigger established; does the conclusion match the rule's right side).
88	4. **The popular trap gets the most attention.** Strong explanations spend their longest wrong-answer note on the choice that drew 15-30% of students and say why it tempts. Loose ones treat it like every other wrong answer.
89	5. **The correct-answer note explains the fit, and often generalizes it.** Strong: "Another correct answer performing the same function could have said..." (PT146-S2-Q13); the karaoke/Jenga reason why A → C still works when you predicted A and B → C (PT139-S4-Q21). Loose: "This is the flaw."
90	6. **Fidelity.** Two loose items are long. Length did not save them: a misquoted answer choice, imported outside facts, or the author disowning the question destroys model value regardless of word count.
91	
92	What did not separate them: word count by itself (PT152-S4-Q19 is 479 words and strong; PT158-S2-Q16 is 716 and loose), the choice-tag vocabulary, and the presence of jokes (several strong ones joke; it only hurts when the joke is at the question's or the answer's expense).
93	
94	## 4. Rubric used for the shortlist
95	
96	Gate (hard requirement): full Argument breakdown and all five choice notes. Every shortlisted candidate passed.
97	
98	Scored 0 / 1 / 2:
99	
100	| Criterion | 2 | 1 | 0 |
101	|---|---|---|---|
102	| **P** Prediction | Goal names the content or shape of the right answer before the choices (may give two acceptable shapes) | Goal names the task or admits it cannot predict | No prediction |
103	| **X** Precision | Names the exact gap, flaw or relationship in this argument's words | Uses the right label but stays generic | Label only, or wrong |
104	| **E** Eliminations | Every wrong answer gets a question-grounded reason; the popular trap gets the most attention | Most do; one or two verdict-only notes | Verdict-only notes |
105	| **T** Transfer | States the move in a form that works on the next question of this type (the test, the rule, the recurring trap) | Transfer is implicit | None |
106	| **C** Clarity | Plain, no padding, no pasted repetition | Some padding or pasted notes | Hard to follow |
107	| **L** Learner value | Teaches what a student needs to understand and repeat the move: why the trap tempts, what to do when stuck, a worked example or analogy, a second correct answer that would also work | Justifies the key well but does not teach the move | Only justifies the key |
108	
109	Plus a **fidelity flag** (not scored; disqualifying if serious): `slip` factual or typo error, `tone` the author mocks the question or answer, `register` explicit-Standard PT157/158 prose, `transcription` damaged text.
110	
111	Learner value is the criterion that most often separated a 10 from a 12. Many explanations justify the key correctly (1) but only the best ones also show the student the move they will reuse (2).
112	
113	## 5. Step structure per family (DB, most common sequences)
114	
115	Labels normalized (parenthetical indicator words dropped: "Conclusion (therefore)" counts as Conclusion).
116	
117	- **Flaw (524):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (67%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (9%); with an Intermediate Conclusion step (5%). Evaluate usually names the famous flaw or builds the objection; Goal often gives both the formal and the "fails to consider" phrasing.
118	- **Must Be True / Most Supported (462):** Statements > Evaluate > Goal (45%); Statements only (16%). Statements is often a diagram. Evaluate scans for conditional, causal, math and pivot wording and names the inference to make.
119	- **Necessary Assumption (352):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (66%). Goal typically offers a linking answer and a defender answer, and says "negate it".
120	- **Weaken (335) / Strengthen (277):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (59% / 55%). Evaluate uses "Given that ... how could we argue that ..." or Curious Fact / Alternate Explanation / Plausibility.
121	- **Paradox (213):** "Apparent Paradox > Evaluate" (22%, older tests) or "Given that ... > How can it be that / Why is it that ... > Evaluate > Goal" (about 30% combined, often with Evaluate / Goal merged).
122	- **Sufficient Assumption (193):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (53%); Evidence > Conclusion > Evaluate (21%). Goal usually diagrams the missing link as the logic path.
123	- **Principle-Strengthen (181):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (48%). Goal is "If evidence, then conclusion" in general terms.
124	- **Principle-Conform (99):** mixed. Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (24%) when the principle is in the answers; Principle > Evaluate > Goal (9%) or Principle > Goal (7%) when it is in the stimulus.
125	- **Main Conclusion (144) / Role (126):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (42% / 53%), with an Intermediate Conclusion step in about 8%. Evaluate almost always opens with the reminder that the conclusion usually comes early (first sentence or after a pivot).
126	- **Parallel (100) / Parallel Flaw (123):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (49% / 67%), Evidence first in about 19-35%. Goal is an algebraic recipe (P1, P2, C) or a plain-language template.
127	- **Agree/Disagree (119):** speaker-named steps. "Speaker's Claims > Speaker's Responses > Goal", often a numbered claim list with "Does [speaker 2] disagree?" per claim.
128	- **Method (91):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (37%) for one speaker; "X's Conclusion > X's Evidence > Y's Response > Evaluate > Goal" for two speakers.
129	- **Evaluate (28):** Conclusion > Evidence > Evaluate > Goal (75%).
130	
131	## 6. Recurring phrasing conventions
132	
133	- **Frames:** "Given that ... how could we (still) argue that ..." (Weaken, Strengthen, NA, Flaw); "Curious Fact ... Author's Explanation ... Alternate Explanation / Plausibility" (any causal argument); "Given that ... How can it be that ..." (Paradox); "If Evidence, then Conclusion" (Principle, SA).
134	- **Tests in choice notes:** negate it and ask if it weakens (NA); "fails to consider X" means ask whether X weakens, "presumes X" means ask whether X is necessary (Flaw); "argues X by Y: X must match the conclusion and Y the evidence" (Method, Flaw); a rule can only prove its right-hand side (Principle, SA); "can we support the Agree position and the Disagree position?" (Agree/Disagree); "Why should we believe this?" (Role, Main Conclusion).
135	- **Recurring trap names:** Too Strong (the most common NA and MBT tag), Out of Scope, No Impact, Unclear Impact, No Distinction (Paradox), Deepens Paradox, Wrong Flaw / Not an Objection (Flaw), Bad Conclusion / Evidence / Trigger Match (Principle, Parallel), Premise / Last-Claim Trap / Opposing Position (Main Conclusion), Unsupported Agree/Disagree Position (Agree/Disagree), Fake Opposite / Illegal Negation (NA), Reversal (SA). Tags come from the local markdown; the dump does not include them.
136	- **Rules stated as rules:** "Most X can only be concluded from a Most X premise"; "some / sometimes / can is almost always wrong on Strengthen, Weaken, Paradox, SA"; "the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence"; "about 85-90% of Main Conclusion questions put the conclusion in the first sentence or after a pivot"; "on EXCEPT, three of the working answers usually work one way and one works another".
137	- **Voice habits that should not be copied:** mocking the question or answer ("hot garbage", "so, so dumb", "unthinkably weird", "dumpster fire"), and the PT157/158 em-dash register.
138	
139	## 7. `Explanations/targeted-questions/`
140	
141	Three files: a README and two explanations, PT134-S1-Q16 (Flaw, belief vs implication, answer A) and PT158-S4-Q24 (Weaken, food-supply chain, answer D). The README says they were "added for LSAT U use when Joshua needs specific questions available outside a full PrepTest-section explanation file."
142	
143	They are not LSAT Lab transcriptions. The layout is different and the parser will not read it: an H1 title, a metadata block (type, answer, LSAT U order, bank file), H2 sections for Stimulus, Prompt, Argument breakdown, **Written explanation** (a free prose section the LSAT Lab files do not have), Answer choice explanations with an italic tag, and a closing **Student takeaway**. The prose is written in LSAT U's own voice: no em-dashes, short sentences, jokes built on the stimulus ("the drug sneaks into the food supply wearing a tiny trench coat", "Still a raccoon in the kitchen"), and a one-line takeaway ("Break the link that matters. That's the move.").
144	
145	Authorship: git attributes them to `vnjkoxpl-42905` (Joshua's account) inside a bulk commit dated 2026-09-05 titled "Update RRC launch page copy", with no co-author line and no other reference in the repo or wiki. The style, the house-voice compliance and the README wording point to an AI assistant (Claude or Codex) drafting them for Joshua. That is an inference; the git history cannot confirm who typed them.
146	
147	They are useful as a picture of the target voice (especially "Written explanation" and "Student takeaway", which have no LSAT Lab equivalent). They are not in the golden set because they are outside the corpus and use a different structure.
148	
149	## 8. Caveats
150	
151	- All scores are one reader's judgment, formed by reading each candidate once. Treat 11 vs 12 as a tie.
152	- Features and difficulty come from the question bank. 110 explained questions have no bank match, and some shortlisted items have no features in the bank at all (PT143-S1-Q6, PT154-S1-Q10), so the coverage grid undercounts them.
153	- I read about 125 explanations in full or near-full, weighted toward the highest-scoring candidates in each family and difficulty band. I did not read all 3,000, so a better exemplar for a thin cell may exist.

17	## What separates a top-tier explanation from a loose one
18	
19	Length does not. These do:
20	
21	1. **The Evaluate step argues with the argument** ("How could we argue the opposite?") instead of restating it.
22	2. **The Goal names the answer** it expects, not just the task.
23	3. **Every wrong answer gets a reason and usually a test.** Loose ones average one verdict-only note per question ("This is irrelevant").
24	4. **The most popular trap gets the most attention.**
25	5. **The correct-answer note teaches the move**, so a student can repeat it on the next question, not just confirm the key.
26	
27	Complete (full breakdown plus all five Choice Notes) is a gate, not a score.

95	## Where the set is thin
96	
97	- **Refute a Conditional**: 14 gap questions, no strong exemplar anywhere in the corpus. The guide for it will lean on the conditional picks (#2, #9, #35, #44) and LSAT Hacks for context.
98	- **Paradox**: hard and EXCEPT now covered by #51 (PT106-S2-Q26).
99	- **Method on Plans / Recommendations** (15 gap questions) has no pick.
100	- Most other empty cells are topic features (Science, Medical) that do not change how an explanation is built, so they do not need their own model.

5	STIMULUS: Medical specialists report that patients with back muscle injuries who receive a combination of drugs and physical therapy do only as well as those who receive physical therapy alone. Yet the specialists state that drugs are a necessary part of the treatment of all patients who receive them for back muscle injuries.
6	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to reconcile the medical specialists' two claims?
7	(A) [3% | No Impact] Medical specialists treat all patients who have serious back muscle injuries with either physical therapy alone or a combination of drugs and physical therapy.
8	(B) [63% | Correct] Medical specialists who prescribe these treatments make accurate judgments about who needs both drugs and physical therapy and who needs physical therapy alone.
9	(C) [4% | Weak / No Impact] Some back muscle injuries have been completely healed by a combination of drugs and physical therapy.
10	(D) [23% | Weak / No Impact] Some back muscle injuries that have been aggravated by improper attempts at physical therapy, such as home massage, have been successfully treated with drugs.
11	(E) [7% | Deepens Paradox] Patients with injuries to other muscles show more improvement when treated with both drugs and physical therapy than when treated with physical therapy alone.
12	--- BREAKDOWN
13	## Background
14	Patients with back injuries who receive a combo of drugs and physical therapy do only as well as those who just receive physical therapy.
15	## Surprising Claim
16	Medical specialists say that the drugs are a necessary part of the treatment for all patients who receive them for back injuries.
17	## Evaluate
18	Given That ... the people who get (physical therapy + drugs) do only as well as the people go get (physical therapy + no drugs)
19	How Can We Say That .... the drugs are a necessary part of the treatment for the people who got it?
20	It looks like the drugs are doing nothing, since people who only had physical therapy are doing just as well, but our correct answer will give us some way to say that the people who got [physical therapy + drugs] wouldn't have done as well if they had only gotten physical therapy (or maybe it will say that the people who didn't get drugs would have done worse if they had been given drugs).
21	## Goal
22	Even though we don't usually try to predict answers for Paradox, those of us who know the Anti-Causal arguments we see in the Assumption family may recognize a pattern here.
23	If I say, "the students who got tutoring scored just as high on the math quiz as the students who didn't get tutoring. Therefore, the tutoring did nothing", how could we object to that argument?
24	We can say, "The students who got tutoring were the ones who were most struggling with math; had they not gotten tutoring, there's no way they would have scored as high as the other students".
25	In other words, when two groups are measured as being the same, if we want to argue that some special property one of them had was meaningful, we have to say that "without it, they wouldn't have been measured the same".
26	--- CHOICE NOTES
27	(A) This doesn't offer any distinction between the two groups (the ones who do / don't receive drugs), so it doesn't provide us way to say that the drug-group really needed those drugs and would have been worse without them.
28	(B) This correct answer is pretty miserable. It's one of those ones where if you weren't already thinking what LSAT wanted you to be thinking, then you wouldn't see anything in this answer to provoke that thinking in you.
29	
30	This is getting at the idea that medical specialists only give physical therapy to patients who can recover with physical therapy alone, whereas they give physical therapy and drugs to patients who would need both in order to recover.
31	
32	So the drugs are a necessary part of the treatment for those who receive it, because the medical specialist accurately judged that those people needed drugs.
33	
34	And drugs were not necessary to those who didn't receive them. If you wanted to, you could put this into conditional form:
35	
36	received drugs → needed drugsdidn't receive drugs → didn't need drugs
37	
38	Even though the patients w/o drugs recovered as much as the patients w/ drugs, the drugs were still necessary for those who got them, because the medical specialist made an accurate judgment in assessing whether that patient needed drugs.
39	
40	If it sounds a little circular, it almost is, but it's really just getting at the simpler real-world idea that a specialist will prescribe physical therapy for milder cases of back injuries and physical therapy + drugs for more severe cases.
41	
42	If she succeeds in getting all her patients to recover, it might look like drugs did nothing, but it could otherwise be that drugs did do something, and she was wise/accurate enough to only give it to the people who actually needed the drugs.
43	(C) Some = at least one, so any answer with "some / sometimes" will almost always be wrong on questions like Paradox, Strengthen, and Weaken (where we want more powerful answers).
44	
45	It's great that some back muscle injury patients completely healed with drugs and physical therapy. According to our background fact, that means that people who got physical therapy alone also completely healed.
46	
47	This doesn't do anything to justify the idea that the drugs were a necessary part of that complete recovery, because it doesn't provide any difference between the people who got drugs and those who didn't.
48	(D) Some = at least one, so any answer with "some / sometimes" will almost always be wrong on questions like Paradox, Strengthen, and Weaken (where we want more powerful answers).
49	
50	This answer is weak and is dealing with a really strange atypical case in which amateur attempts at physical therapy messed up the patient's condition and then drugs successfully treated that condition.
51	
52	The fact that drugs helped these specific people who initially had their injuries made worse by improper physical therapy doesn't help us to explain this big, generalized data set in the first sentence:
53	
54	ALL the back injury patients who received physical therapy + drugs did only as well as those who got only physical therapy.
55	
56	The "some" people in this answer that were successfully treated with drugs still did only as well as some other people who were only treated with physical therapy. So why were drugs necessary?
57	
58	Maybe proper attempts at physical therapy would have been just as effective?
59	(E) The reference to "other injuries" is out of scope to begin with, but then this answer continues to get worse, as it makes it sound like physical therapy + drugs is better than physical therapy alone.

61	Okay, well then I'm even more confused why in the case of back muscle injuries, physical therapy + drugs only did as well as PT alone.

64	=================== PT152-S2-Q17 | diff 4 | key B | score 22.3 | features: -
65	STIMULUS: Scientist: A number of errors can plague a data-collection process. Since examining the collected data enables researchers to detect many of these errors, it is standard practice for researchers to correct collected data. However, in my field, there is a striking tendency for such corrections to favor Jones’s theory; that is, the majority of corrections result in the corrected data’s being closer than the uncorrected data to what Jones’s theory predicts.
66	STEM: Which one of the following, if true, most helps to explain the tendency of corrections in the scientist’s field to favor Jones’s theory?
67	(A) [5% | No Impact] Researchers normally give data that is in line with a theory the same weight as data that conflicts with that theory when they are determining whether to accept that theory.
68	(B) [67% | Correct] Researchers in the scientist’s field give data that conflicts with Jones’s theory greater scrutiny than they give data that is in line with Jones’s theory.
69	(C) [18% | Unclear Impact] Researchers in the scientist’s field are more likely to pursue lines of research that they expect will favor theories they accept than to pursue other lines of research.
70	(D) [5% | Very Weak] Even if researchers fail to detect errors in a data-collection process when they examine the data that they collected, that does not guarantee that no such errors exist.
71	(E) [4% | No Impact] Researchers in the scientist’s field have formulated several other theories that attempt to explain the same range of phenomena that Jones’s theory attempts to explain.
72	--- BREAKDOWN
73	## Background
74	Data-collection is plagued by erroneous data points and researchers examine collected data for errors so that they can correct them.
75	## How Can We Explain
76	Most corrections result in the data point becoming closer to what Jones's theory would predict.
77	## Evaluate
78	Let's say that Jones's theory predicts a value of 10 for each data point.
79	  We get a bunch of data points:
80	  (10, 10, 7, 8, 13, 10, 10, 11, 15, 6, 10, 9)
81	  Some of them turn out to be wrong, and when we fix them, they tend to move closer to Jones's predictions.
82	  For example, we find out that the 7 is an incorrect data point. In reality it should be 8.
83	  We find out that the 13 is an incorrect data point. In reality it should be 12.
84	  How come the corrections are shifting closer to Jones's prediction of 10? Shouldn't the errors be random? The 13 was actually lower than it should have been? Or the 7 was higher that it should have been?
85	  What accounts for the fact that corrections usually bring us closer to Jones's estimate?
86	## Goal
87	My first thought was "Maybe Jones's theory is correct! A correct theory makes correct predictions. Data points should align with the predictions. An erroneous data point, when corrected, will move closer to a correct theory, since a correct theory makes correct predictions."
88	  However, I know not to get too much anchor bias to my anticipated answer on Paradox questions, since the correct answer is frequently surprising.
89	--- CHOICE NOTES
90	(A) We don't care about whether a theory is going to get accepted or not. We just care about why corrected data points are generally drifting towards Jones's theory.
91	(B) This points to a distinction that could be a causal difference-maker. If the people scanning for errors are giving more scrutiny to data points that conflict with Jones's theory, then they won't notice erroneous data points that align with Jones's theory but are actually bad data points. Those data points would have shifted away from Jones's theory.
92	
93	This is more or less describing confirmation bias skewing the results of a study.
94	
95	If a high school teacher thought that male students were more likely to plagiarize than were female students, he might give the essays from male students more scrutiny, in terms of checking online to see if they seemed to have plagiarized any of their papers.
96	
97	Since he doesn't expect the female students to plagiarize, he's not checking their papers as closely.
98	
99	Through this act of confirmation bias, he will reinforce this stereotype he has. He'll find more examples of males plagiarizing than females plagiarizing, since he's putting more effort into looking for trouble with males' papers.
100	
101	Similarly, if these researchers already implicitly assume J's theory is right, they'll have some confirmation bias. They won't bother checking the the data points that align with Jones's theory, because they're assuming those data points are probably correct.
102	
103	Meanwhile, they'll expend effort looking at data points that diverge from Jones's theory, so they'll catch more offenders (erroneous data points).
104	(C) Someone might have liked this if they were looking for an answer that said, "the corrected data points shift towards Jones's theory, because Jones's theory is right".
105	
106	This is more like, "Some lines of research will favor Jones's theory. These researchers accept Jones's theory, so they pursue flattering lines of research. Thus, since Jones's theory is 'right' for these lines of research, corrections will usually bring data points closer to Jones's predictions."
107	
108	There's a lot of stretching for us to try to turn this answer into that story. The biggest gap is that we have no idea whether any researchers in this field favor Jones's theory, and without knowing that we can't possibly get this answer to go anywhere.
109	(D) "Even if ... that does not guarantee" is language that shows there is a possibility for at least one thing. Even if they fail to detect errors, it is possible that at least one error exists.
110	
111	"At least one" strength of language (some, sometimes, not all, can, may, might, need not) is almost never correct on Strengthen / Weaken / Paradox.
112	
113	This answer has no power to make a distinction between data points that do / don't align with Jones's predictions.
114	(E) The fact that Jones's theory is not the only theory doesn't change anything, and it doesn't explain why corrected data points are mainly moving in the direction of aligning more closely with Jones's theory.

380	STIMULUS: Economist: Although average hourly wages vary
381	considerably between different regions of this
382	country, in each region, the average hourly
383	wage for full-time jobs increased last year.
384	Paradoxically, however, in the country as a
385	whole, the average hourly wage for full-time jobs
386	decreased last year.
387	STEM: Which one of the following, if true of the economist’s
388	country, most helps to resolve the apparent paradox in
389	the economist’s statements?
390	(A) [2% | No Impact] In the country as a whole, the average hourly wage for full-time jobs has decreased slightly for each of the last three years.
391	(B) [83% | Correct] Last year, to reduce costs, employers moved many full-time jobs from regions with relatively high hourly wages to regions where those jobs typically pay much less.
392	(C) [2% | No Impact] The year before last, the unemployment rate reached a ten-year low; last year, however, the unemployment rate increased slightly.
393	(D) [8% | No Impact] Last year, the rate at which the average hourly wage for full-time jobs increased varied considerably between different regions of the country.
394	(E) [4% | No Impact] Last year, hourly wages for most full-time jobs in the manufacturing sector declined while those for most full-time jobs in the service sector increased.
395	--- BREAKDOWN
396	## Given that ...
397	In every region of the country, the average hourly wage for full-time jobs increased last year.
398	## How is it that ...
399	In the country as a whole, the average hourly wage for full-time jobs decreased last year?
400	## Evaluate
401	This is a tricky mathematical paradox. If the average wage went up in each region, then how could the average wage go down nationally?
402	Normally, we don't spend too much time up front on Paradox questions trying to guess the answer because there could potentially be a lot of ways to resolve the Paradox. But when it's mathematical, there is often only one way.
403	In this case, in order for every region's average to go up while the national average went down, we need there to be a net effect of people moving out of regions whose average is higher than the national average into areas whose average is lower than the national average.
404	Say that the average wage nationally is $25 / hr. Some regions, like San Francisco, will have regional average wage of $30 / hr. Some regions like, like Oklahoma City, will have a regional average wage of $20 / hr.
405	Let's say, last year, OKC's average rose to $21 / hr and SF's average rose to $31 / hr.
406	We'd expect the national average to start creeping towards $26 / hr. But now consider the effect if tons of people move from SF to OKC.
407	Someone who used to be making $30 / hr in SF now lives in Austin and makes $21 / hr.
408	When it comes to the national average hourly wage, people who made $30 / hr last year but make $21 / hr this year are going to bring that average down.
409	Or in case this makes more sense, suppose that SF is a region with a $30 / hr average wage. OKC is a region with a $20 / hr average wage. Bob lives in SF and makes $28 / hr.
410	Now he moves to OKC and makes $25 / hr. What has he done to the average wage in SF, OKC, and nationally?
411	He raised the average wage in SF, because if the average is 30 and you get rid of a data point that is 28, then that will boost the average. (If you kick a teenager off your team, then the average age of your team will go up).
412	He raised the average wage in OKC, because the average there was $20, but he makes $25. And he lowered the national average, because he went from making $28 to $25.
413	## Goal
414	We need to hear that people moved out of higher paying areas into lower paying areas.
415	--- CHOICE NOTES
416	(A) We don't care whether 2 and 3 years ago the national average also decreased. We're trying to explain how this past year the national average could have decreased, given that this past year, every regional average increased.
417	(B) This is what we were looking for. We need people from areas that pay higher than the national average to move to areas that pay lower than the national average.
418	
419	That's the only mathematical way we can get every region's average to increase while the overall average decreases.
420	
421	This answer doesn't in any way "settle the math" and guarantee a lower national average, but it reflects the right type of movement that could potentially reconcile the math paradox.
422	(C) We don't care about the unemployment rate. We're only looking at a paradox that deals with average hourly wages, so by definition we only care about those who are employed.

432	STIMULUS: If grain prices double then the average price of a loaf of bread will rise between 10 and 15 percent, whereas the price of grain-fed beef will come close to doubling.
433	STEM: Which one of the following would, if true, most contribute to an explanation of the phenomenon described above?

437	(D) [4% | No Distinction] Only an insignificantly small proportion of the beef sold in retail markets is produced from cattle fed on grass rather than grain.

442	## Why is it that ...

444	## Evaluate
445	We really could have framed this paradox either way, switching these two ingredients. We're really just trying to explain why a doubling in the price of grain would have a smaller effect on the price of bread than it would on the price of grain-fed beef. Generally speaking, if you make a certain product, you have a lot of different expenses that go into setting your price. If one of those expenses (such as grain) were to rise by 30%, it wouldn't necessarily make the price of your product go up by 30%, because grain might be a very part of the expense to make your product. For example, if strawberries rose in price, it might only modestly affect the price of a mixed berry yogurt, because there are other fruits in there as well, whereas it might more drastically affect the price of strawberry yogurt.
446	## Goal
447	Look for an answer saying that grain is a more important ingredient / bigger category of expense when it comes to grain-fed beef than when it comes to a loaf of bread.

449	(A) An answer will be worthless to us if it doesn't provide a distinction between bread and beef, when it comes to use of grain.
450	This answer only talks about beef.

453	(C) This provides the distinction we were looking for, where we learn that grain is a more important ingredient / bigger expense category when it comes to beef than when it comes to bread.

455	Suppose their expense breakdown is like this:

457	$0.20 - yeast
458	$0.20 - sugar
459	$0.30 - eggs
460	$1.50 - labor / marketing
461	$2.50 - total expenses
462	If grain doubles, it'll mean that the grain part now costs 60 cents. So now the total expense will be $2.80. The bread maker might need to raise the price of bread to like $3.30 to still make the same profit. That was a 10% increase in price.
463	Meanwhile, for beef, let's say a certain quantity of beef costs $3 and the expense breakdown is as follows:
464	$2.00 - grain

466	$0.25 - distribution

468	If the cost of grain doubles, it will now be $4, and so the total expenses will be $4.50 per unit of beef. That means the cost of beef will now need to be like $5 per unit to make the same profit. The price came close to doubling.

522	STIMULUS: In a poll of eligible voters conducted on the eve of a mayoral election, more of those polled stated that they favored Panitch than stated that they favored any other candidate. Despite this result, another candidate, Yeung, defeated Panitch by a comfortable margin.
523	STEM: Each of the following, if true, contributes to a resolution of the discrepancy described above except:
524	(A) [71% | Deepens the Paradox] Of Yeung's supporters, a smaller percentage were eligible to vote than the percentage of Panitch's supporters who were eligible to vote.
525	(B) [12% | Helps Explain] A third candidate, Mulhern, conducted a press conference on the morning of the election and withdrew from the race.
526	(C) [4% | Helps Explain] The poll's questions were designed by staff members of Panitch's campaign.
527	(D) [4% | Helps Explain] Of the poll respondents supporting Yeung, 70 percent described the election as "important" or "very important," while 30 percent of respondents supporting Panitch did the same.
528	(E) [8% | Helps Explain] The poll, conducted on a Monday, surveyed persons in the downtown area, and the percentage of Yeung's supporters who work downtown is lower than that of Panitch's supporters.
529	--- BREAKDOWN
530	## Given that
531	On a poll of eligible voters, taken the night before the election, Panitch got the most #1 votes on favorability.
532	## How can it be that
533	Yeung beat Panitch by a comfortable margin?
534	## Evaluate
535	We basically need to explain why the survey suggested P would be the winner while reality made Y the winner. Usually, on EXCEPT questions, 3 of the 4 answers we're eliminating come at the stimulus from one angle, while the 4th wrong answer takes a different tactic.
536	In this case, there are a couple ways we could explain the disconnect between the survey and reality.
537	1. The survey was crappy data in that the responses weren't accurate.
538	2. The survey was crappy data in that the respondents weren't a representative sample of people who would be voting in the election.
539	2. The survey was good data at the time, but something suddenly changed between when the poll was taken and when the election was held.
540	## Goal
541	Eliminate four answers that can explain the mismatch between a survey won by Panitch and an election won by Yeung.
542	The correct answer might deepen the paradox, or more likely it will just be neutral.
543	--- CHOICE NOTES

549	(B) Our job here, "Given that Panitch was leading in the poll, how did Yeung win the election?"

551	This answer says, "Yeung won because a third candidate dropped out, and (so all their voters switched to Yeung)."

The results: 40% - Trump 35% - Biden 15% - Yang 10% - others, combined That makes it seem like Trump will win. But if Yang realizes he has no chance and drops out the morning of the election (publicly, via press conference), then Yang voters will have to decide whether to vote for Biden or Trump, and that could make a previous underdog like Biden still win the election by a large margin.

577	=================== PT140-S1-Q20 | diff 5 | key C | score 18.5 | features: Part vs. Whole, Worst of the Worst
578	STIMULUS: Adjusted for inflation, the income earned from wool sales by a certain family of Australian sheep farmers grew substantially during the period from 1840 to 1860. This is because the price for wool sold on the international market was higher than the price paid on domestic markets and the percentage and amount of its wool that this family sold internationally increased dramatically during that period. But even though the family generated more income from selling their wool, they failed to enjoy a commensurate increase in prosperity.
579	STEM: Which one of the following would, if true, help most to resolve the apparent paradox described above?
580	(A) [18% | Out of Scope: end of the 1800s] At the end of the 1800s, prices in general in Australia rose more rapidly than did the wholesale price of wool sold domestically.
581	(B) [10% | No Impact] The prices of wool sold to domestic markets by Australian sheep farmers decreased dramatically during the period in question.
582	(C) [45% | Correct] The international and domestic prices for mutton, sheepskins, and certain other products produced by all Australian sheep farmers fell sharply during the period in question.
583	(D) [20% | No Impact (Tries to Cheat Paradox)] Competition in wool sales increased during the period in question, leaving Australian wool producers in a less favorable position than previously.
584	(E) [6% | No Impact] Among Australian sheep farmers, the percentage who made their living exclusively from international wool sales increased significantly during the period in question.
585	--- BREAKDOWN
586	## Background
587	1840-1860 - the price for wool on the world market was higher than the price paid in domestic markets - this family sold more wool on the world market - this family made a lot more money from wool sales
588	## Surprising Claim
589	During this period, the family didn't see much, if any, increase in prosperity.
590	## Evaluate
591	Hmm, tricky one. A lot of background details to carefully absorb. Given That The family made much more money from wool sales How Can We Explain The family didn't see much of any increase in prosperity? We don't try to predict specific answers on Paradox, since the correct answer often takes a very surprising angle. However, we can think through some general types of ways that a family could make much more money on wool but not see an increase in prosperity: - maybe money isn't worth as much as it used to be? Inflation could mean that even though you make more money, your spending power (and thus prosperity) hasn't gone up. Wait a sec, the first sentence says "Adjusted for inflation", so we can't actually explain it that way. - maybe they faced financial difficulties of some sort? We could make more money from our business but have to spend more money on parking tickets / medical bills / funerals for family members, etc.
592	## Goal
593	Look for an answer that gives us a way to explain their lack of prosperity.
594	--- CHOICE NOTES
595	(A) The period from 1840-1860 would not be described as the end of the 1800s, so it seems like this answer is just completely out of scope.
596	Had this been the right time period, it's still not that useful. This family was selling its wool more internationally, so what's going on with domestic wool prices is pretty irrelevant.
597	(B) It's an established fact that this family made more money from wool sales, and we know that they were selling way more wool internationally than domestically.
598	So the falling price of domestic wool is not super impactful (since this family's fortunes were tied increasingly to the international wool market), and it can't possibly explain a financial loss, since we already know that the family had a financial gain from wool sales.
599	(C) The lovable part of this answer is its strong language (which we want on Paradox, Strengthen, and Weaken): stuff produced by all sheep farmers fell sharply during this period.
600	What makes this answer hard to interpret at first is that we have to realize that "a family of sheep farmers" doesn't necessarily just sell wool as its source of income.
601	Wool is the sheep's fur, which can be sold (and it grows back). But you can also eventually slaughter your sheep and sell its meat (mutton) and its skin (sheepskins).
602	So if you're a family of sheep farmers, you sell a few "products": sheep's wool, sheep's meat, sheep's skin, and possibly other products.
603	All sheep farmers sell this gamut of products, according to this answer choice
604	If sales of wool went up, but sales of all the other sheep products fell sharply, then we have a way to make sense of how this family could make way more money from wool sales while still not having a big uptick in prosperity.
605	(D) In order for this answer to help us explain the lack of increase in prosperity, we'd have to interpret it to mean that "higher competition in wool sales led to worse sales for the family of sheep farmers, which is why they didn't have an increase in prosperity".
606	But we can't draw that sort of story out of this answer because the background facts already established that this family earned a lot more money from wool sales than before.
607	In a sense, any answer about wool sales is doomed, because that part of the paradox is already locked in as a financial gain.
608	(E) I can't see any way we would take this demographic fact about sheep farmers (a higher proportion of them made all their money from international wool sales) and turn it into a reason that this family is not seeing an uptick in prosperity.
609	We don't have any way of knowing whether they make their money exclusively from the international wool market, and even if they did, we've heard that the international market is booming, so that wouldn't be an effective mechanism for explaining a lack of prosperity.

674	## Why is it that …

15	## Evidence

But we're not alive during that period (there are not currently trillions of humans colonizing the galaxy).
17	## Evaluate
18	This is a real crappy argument, but our job on Method isn't to evaluate the argument. In real life, smart tech people like Elon Musk make arguments similar to this to argue that we probably live in a simulation: if there were simulated universes, there would be way more simulated universes than real universes, so the probability would be much higher that you live in a simulated universe than in the real universe. Thus, you probably live in a simulated universe. On Method, our job is just to characterize the type of evidence used or the type of move the author made, and the correct answer usually sounds like one of these options: analogy, rule out alternatives, implications of logic, example / counterexample, definition / distinction, overlooked consideration, dubious assumption, alternative explanation The key moment that would stick out to me in this argument is the moment of "Suppose they're right ...." / "If that were really so ... " This is what we mean when we say "implications of logic". The second half of an If/Then statement is called its outcome, its consequence, its necessary implication. So authors will either say, "Let's follow the logic of your plan or your rule ... it ultimately ends at this undesirable place, so it looks like the plan / rule is no good". Or, like we're doing in this argument, authors will say, "If you were right, then X would follow. But X isn't true. So you must be wrong." This 2nd style is arguing via contrapositive logic. If you were right → implication X But implication X is not the case. Thus, you are not right.

We might accidentally mess up what makes us otherwise so wonderful." We're obviously filling in some blanks there, but that's how one would charitably interpret this argument. It's sort of like when you tell someone, "I know you wish you could go back in time to fix that mistake you made, but your mistakes made you who you are today, and so we have to accept the bad with the good." In terms of our job on Method, we're just supposed to try to characterize the type of evidence or reasoning employed. 223	(B) [60% | Correct] stating the definition of a key term of the argument
224	(C) [1% | Bad Premise Match] using statistical findings to dispute a claim
225	(D) [29% | Bad Premise Match] undermining a claim by showing that the claim is self-contradictory
226	(E) [2% | Out of Scope: empirical evidence / Out of Scope: support on over another] using empirical evidence to support one definition of a key term of the argument over another
227	--- BREAKDOWN
228	## Conclusion
229	It's absurd to say that people are morally obligated to act in way X, because acting in way not-X would be unnatural.
230	## Evidence
231	An unnatural action is either a violation of the laws of nature, or a statistical anomaly.
232	You can't act in a way that violates the laws of nature, and the fact that something isn't usually done isn't a good reason not to do it.
233	## Evaluate
234	There aren't any keywords indicating the conclusion, so we need to mainly identify it from the fact that the first sentence has the opinion of a Rebuttal. Since our author is saying, "people claiming X are being absurd", she will need to defend that position, and the rest of the paragraph tries to defend that position.
235	Since this argument is pretty hard to understand, it might help to come up with an example of something where "acting that way would be unnatural".
236	Maybe we could say "giving your Dad flowers on Mother's Day would be unnatural". According to this position the philosopher is fighting, we are morally obligated to avoid giving our Dad flowers on Mother's Day because doing so would be unnatural.
237	That does sound absurd!
238	The philosopher digs in to this concept of "unnatural". She's like, which sense of "unnatural" do we mean:
239	1. unnatural, like transcending natural law? outside the realm of physics? or 2. unnatural, like 'weird, not customary'?
240	Her final sentence is exploring both of those options and saying, if we mean #1, then the original position is saying, "you're morally obligated to follow the physical laws of the universe". The author is like, "That's absurd. We don't need to be morally warned against doing something impossible."
241	if we mean #2, then the original position is saying, "you're morally obligated to only do normal, customary things. Don't be weird or different." And the author is like, "That's absurd, the fact that something is weird or uncustomary doesn't provide a good reason to avoid doing it."
242	## Goal
243	How would we describe the argument in general terms?
244	The author sort of creates an either / or (does "unnatural" mean impossible or unusual) and then follows both roads to say, "either way you slice it, this original claim is absurd".
245	We might seen language like the author considers two options and **rules them out**. Or we might see something like, "the author considers different implications of a claim to show that they all lead to absurdity".
246	--- CHOICE NOTES
247	(A) The author's conclusion is undermining a concept. What is that concept?
248	
249	you're morally obligated to act naturally
250	
251	Does the author ever say that if we accepted that "we are all morally obligated to act naturally" that this would violate a law of nature?
252	
253	No. The author brought up violating laws of nature when she was interpreting "act naturally" to mean "don't violate a law of nature".
254	(B) Yes, this happens (which is our only metric of right / wrong). The author states the definition of "an unnatural action".
255	
256	She says, "an unnatural action is either a violation of the laws of nature or a statistical anomaly."
257	(C) The author is trying to dispute a claim, the claim that "we're morally obligated to act naturally". Does she ever discuss any statistical findings in the evidence? Heck no.
258	(D) The author is undermining a claim, the claim that "we're morally obligated to act naturally".
259	
260	Does the author do so by showing that the claim is self-contradictory, that by acting naturally we would be doing the opposite of what we're morally obligated to do?
261	
262	No. The author doesn't try to argue that the claim contradicts itself. The author is arguing the claim is absurd, which is synonym for "dumb / meaningless / silly". A self-contradiction is a very specific hard-to-achieve kind of thing.
263	
264	SELF-CONTRADICTION I am not speaking English right now. The Sun always rises in the East, except today it rose in the West
265	(E) There isn't any empirical evidence discussed (observable measurable data). The author provides two possible definitions for the key term "unnatural action", but she never argues for one over the other. She just shows how whichever definition you choose, you still get an absurd original position.
266	
267	Also, amusingly, we could never pick this answer since it implies (B). Clearly, according to this answer choice, the argument did define a key term, so we couldn't pick (E) and eliminate (B).

270	=================== PT147-S4-Q12 | diff 5 | key E | score 18.9 | features: 2-Speaker, Math-y Stuff, Rebuttals
271	STIMULUS: West: Of our company’s three quality control inspectors, Haynes is clearly the worst. Of the appliances that were returned to us last year because of quality control defects, half were inspected by Haynes.
272	Young: But Haynes inspects significantly more than half the appliances we sell each year.
273	STEM: Young responds to West’s argument by
274	(A) [3% | Wrong Flaw] contending that the argument presupposes what it is trying to prove
275	(B) [12% | Relevance vs. Accuracy] questioning the relevance of West’s conclusion
276	(C) [28% | Doesn't Dispute Premise] disputing the accuracy of one of the argument’s stated premises
277	(D) [7% | Too Weak] arguing for a less extreme version of West’s conclusion
278	(E) [49% | Correct] denying one of the argument’s presuppositions
279	--- BREAKDOWN
280	## West's Conclusion
281	Haynes is the worst of our three quality control inspectors.
282	## West's Evidence
283	Half of the appliances that were returned to us last year for being defective were inspected by Haynes.
284	## Young's Response
285	Yeah, but Haynes does way more than half of the appliances.
286	## Evaluate
287	This is a response that's talking about proportionality. All other things being equal, if all three quality control inspectors were equally good at their jobs, then they would each be responsible for 33.3% of the items returned because of defects. They would always be tied in terms of, "which inspector had the most of their items returned for defects". However, let's say they are equally good, but Haynes inspects 50% of the items, Rocco does 25%, and T-Bone does 25%. If it turns out that Haynes is "the inspector who had the most of his items returned for defects", is it fair for Rocco and T-bone to tease him? No, because of course Haynes will have the most items returned. He's inspected twice as many items as those rascals, so he has way more items out there in the world, with his name on it as the quality control inspector. If Haynes inspects 50% of the items, and he's equally good as the other two, then we would expect 50% of the returned items to be from Haynes. If someone does better than their percent, it shows they're better than average. If Rocco inspects 25% of the items but is only responsible for 20% of the returned items, that means that Rocco is outperforming others.
288	## Goal
289	Young responds by Introducing a New Consideration, i.e. bringing up important information that makes us see the first person's evidence in a different light. When we were assuming the work was divided 1/3, 1/3, 1/3 among the three inspectors, we would have assumed that each inspector would have inspected 1/3 of the items that got returned. Since Haynes inspected 1/2 of the items returned, he looks like he's doing worse than his fair share. But Young is saying, actually he's doing better than his fair share. He inspects like more than 65% of these items, and he's only responsible for 50% of the returned items.
290	--- CHOICE NOTES
291	(A) This describes the famous Circular Argument flaw:
292	- conclusion restates the evidence
293	- argument assumes the truth of the conclusion
294	Young is not saying that West's argument was circular. He's just saying, "You have to know the initial reference point here. Haynes inspects way more items than the other two inspectors do."
295	(B) Young doesn't question the relevance of the conclusion, just the accuracy.
296	She is suggesting to West that Haynes is actually doing better than the other two, since he's responsible for inspecting way more than half the items, and only half the items returned went through him.
297	(C) In almost all these 2-Speaker Method questions they have this trap answer. We know that on LSAT, people almost never deny the truth of a premise.
298	They challenge assumptions. They provide other information that re-casts that premise in a new light. But they don't say "No" to a premise.
299	Young does not dispute that half of the returned items were inspected by Haynes.
300	(D) Young does not actually explicitly conclude anything. The new context she presents would lead someone mathematically to infer that Haynes is actually outperforming his other inspectors. If he's inspecting 65% of the items but is only responsible for 50% of the returned items, he's doing better than average.
301	So Young's response is not suggesting a less extreme version. It would be suggesting a contradictory version: Haynes is definitely not the worst of the 3 (he may even be the best)
302	(E) In order for West to think it's bad that Haynes is responsible for 50% of the returned items, he has to be assuming that Haynes inspected less than 50% of the original items that got sold.
303	Presumably, West is assuming that because there are three quality control inspectors, they're each responsible for about 33.3% of the appliances sold.
304	You need to assume that Haynes is inspecting less than 50% of the items in order to see that 50% of the returned items went through him and think, "He seems disproportionately bad. He's clearly the worst!"
305	Young says that Haynes is inspecting way more than 50% of the items, so she is denying the assumption that Haynes is inspecting less than 50% of the items.
306

308	=================== PT140-S1-Q9 | diff 5 | key A | score 18.3 | features: Missing Link / Idea, Explain Curious Fact
309	STIMULUS: Records from 1850 to 1900 show that in a certain region, babies’ birth weights each year varied with the success of the previous year’s crops: the more successful the crops, the higher the birth weights. This indicates that the health of a newborn depends to a large extent on the amount of food available to the mother during her pregnancy.
310	STEM: The argument proceeds by
311	(A) [51% | Correct] inferring from a claimed correlation between two phenomena that two other phenomena are causally connected to one another
312	(B) [19% | Bad Conclusion Match] inferring from the claim that two phenomena have fluctuated together that one of those phenomena must be the sole cause of the other
313	(C) [11% | Bad Conclusion Match] inferring from records concerning a past correlation between two phenomena that that correlation still exists
314	(D) [12% | Bad Conclusion Match] inferring from records concerning two phenomena the existence of a common cause of the phenomena and then presenting a hypothesis about that common cause
315	(E) [7% | Bad Premise Match] inferring the existence of one causal connection from that of another and then providing an explanation for the existence of the two causal connections
316	--- BREAKDOWN
317	## Conclusion
318	The health of a newborn depends to a large extent on the amount of food available to the mother during pregnancy.
319	## Evidence
320	From 1850 to 1900, babies' birth weights varied with the success of the previous year's crops: more successful crops were associated with higher birth weights.
321	## Evaluate
322	Our job on Method questions isn't to evaluate the soundness of the argument, so if you're thinking, "Whoa, dude ... health of a newborn isn't the same as birth weight", you're correct, but call off your dogs. We're not here to fight the logic, just to describe it. Hopefully, though, our familiarity with causal arguments provides us with some mental vocabulary for describing the move from evidence to conclusion. The evidence provides a correlation (X varies with Y ... the more X, the more Y). And the conclusion posits some causal relationship (X depends to a large extent on Y).
323	## Goal
324	We just want an answer that descriptively matches what we saw. We can anticipate that the answer might sound something like "the author concludes a causal relationship between two things based on some study data that shows a correlation between two things".
325	--- CHOICE NOTES
326	(A) When answers say "inferring from Y that X", the Y would be the Evidence and the thing inferred, X, would be the Conclusion.
327	So, was the Evidence a "claimed correlation between two phenomena"? Yes. The evidence claimed a correlation between birth weight and success of crops.
328	Does the conclusion say that two other phenomena are causally connected? Yes. It says that health of a newborn depends strongly on food available to mother.
329	In a weird way, noticing those language shifts from Evidence to Conclusion (i.e. going from talking about "birth weight" to "health of a newborn", or from "success of crops" to "food available to mom") is helping us to understand in this answer choice why it says two other phenomena.
330	If we can match it up, it's correct. And we can match this up.
331	(B) We can match this Premise half of this answer up with the Evidence, since "the more successful the crops, the higher the birth weights" is claiming that two phenomena have fluctuated together.
332	But that would mean, according to this answer choice, that our conclusion said, "Therefore, birth weight is caused solely by the success of crops".
333	Not only did the conclusion actually introduce different concepts, not the same two phenomena from the evidence, it also hedges its wording and says "X depends to a large extent on Y", not "Y is the sole cause of X".
334	(C) We can latch on to the Premise half of this answer choice; there were records concerning a past correlation between "birth weight" and "crop success".
335	But then, according to this answer choice, the conclusion should have sounded like, "This indicates that birth weight and crop success are still associated with each other".
336	That's not a good match for our actual conclusion, which is making a causal (not correlative) claim, and which is talking about things beyond "birth weight" and "crop success".
337	(D) The evidence definitely had records concerning two phenomena: birth weight / crop success.
338	Does the conclusion say that there is some thing that is causing both birth weight and crop success? No. So we can eliminate from here.
339	Bizarrely, this answer choice goes on to describe yet another Conclusion. This answer acts like the argument had a Premise -> Intermediate Conclusion -> Main Conclusion structure, which it didn't.
340	(E) If an answer says that we "infer X from Y", then the thing being inferred, X, is a Conclusion and Y is its Evidence.
341	Did we conclude a causal connection? Yes. We concluded that "newborn's health is causally connected to amount of food available to mom during pregnancy".
342	Did the evidence give us another causal connection? No. It just gave us a correlation. Two things "varied" with each other.
343	This is enough to eliminate this answer, but it also goes on to act like there's yet another conclusion, one that says "Here is why X and Y are causally connected and why A and B are causally connected."

353	(C) [2% | Bad Evidence Match] claiming that Carl's argument should be rejected because it is based on an appeal to sentimentality rather than on reasoned principles
354	(D) [11% | Opposite] drawing an analogy that illustrates a major flaw in Carl's argument

398	=================== PT144-S2-Q8 | diff 2 | key E | score 17.2 | features: Explain Curious Fact, Ruling Out Options
399	STIMULUS: Merton: A study showed that people who live on very busy streets have higher rates of heart disease than average. I conclude that this elevated rate of heart disease is caused by air pollution from automobile exhaust.
400	Ortiz: Are you sure? Do we know whether people living on busy streets have other lifestyle factors that are especially conducive to heart disease?
401	STEM: Ortiz criticizes Merton's argument by
402	(A) [2% | No Match: impugns validity of evidence] raising a question about the validity of the study that Merton cites
403	(B) [1% | No Match: other effects of pollution] contending that Merton needs to take into account other effects of air pollution
404	(C) [1% | Out of Scope: misunderstands] claiming that Merton misunderstands a crucial aspect of the study’s findings
405	(D) [5% | Out of Scope: counterexample] raising a counterexample to the general conclusion that Merton draws
406	(E) [90% | Correct] suggesting that alternative explanations for the study’s findings need to be ruled out
407	--- BREAKDOWN
408	## Merton's Conclusion (I conclude)
409	Air pollution from auto exhaust causes an elevated rate of heart disease.
410	## Merton's Evidence
411	A study showed that people who live on very busy streets have higher rates of heart disease than average.
412	## Ortiz's Rebuttal
413	Are you use the heart disease is from auto exhaust? Could it be that people who live on busy streets have other lifestyle factors that would lead to more heart disease?
414	## Evaluate
415	As soon as an LSAT student reads that first sentence, she should be thinking, "This is a study presenting a correlation. The author will probably assume or state some causal explanation for this correlation in just a sec. I should organize this with my Explain Curious Fact mindset."
416	  - Curious Fact ... why is there a correlation between living on busy streets and having higher rates of heart disease?
417	  - Author's Explanation ... living near busy streets is causing the higher rate of heart disease (via air pollution from auto exhaust).
418	  - In the Assumption Family, we're supposed to react to this by thinking
419	    1. Could there be some Alternate Explanation for the curious fact?
420	    2. How plausible is the author's explanation?
421	  - Whenever we deal with a correlation between X and Y, which our author interprets to mean X causes Y, we're supposed to always think of these same two alternate explanations:
422	    - Y causes X (reverse causality)
423	    - Z explains X and Y (3rd factor)
424	  - Ortiz responds with a 3rd factor style objection. He's saying what if there is some other trait that correlates with living on busy streets, and that trait is the causal factor that's leading to higher risk of heart disease. For example, maybe people who live on busy streets are much more likely to be cigarette smokers, or heavy drinkers, or heavy eaters. That would explain why busy streets and heart disease are correlated, even though it's not the busy street's fault.
425	## Goal
426	Look for something like, "Ortiz is suggesting an alternate explanation for the correlation Merton cites between busy streets and heart disease".
427	--- CHOICE NOTES
428	(A) In all the Method of Response questions, there is almost always an answer choice saying that the 2nd person "denies the evidence" offered by the 1st person. But so far, that's never been correct. It's not very LSAT-style to deny someone's evidence.
429	
430	Ortiz isn't saying the study is invalid. He's saying there could be other possible ways to explain its correlation than the way that Merton has latched onto.
431	(B) Ortiz isn't saying that Merton needs to account for other effects of air pollution. He's saying that Merton needs to account for other possible causes of why busy streets could be correlated with heart disease.
432	(C) The only finding from the study is that busy streets are correlated with higher rates of heart disease. Ortiz never "claims" that Merton misunderstood the correlation. Ortiz is asking whether Merton has arrived at a hasty conclusion, on the basis of the correlation.
433	
434	But jumping overconfidently to a conclusion on the basis of evidence X does not mean that you've misunderstood a crucial aspect of evidence X. We could say Merton misunderstand what can / can't be confidently inferred from the study's findings. But that's different, and Ortiz never explicitly claimed Merton misunderstood something, as this answer says.
435	(D) Merton's conclusion is not exactly general, because he says "this elevated rate of heart disease". He's speaking specifically about the people in this study. Even if we grant that the conclusion is general, a counterexample to that conclusion would be data points of people who were exposed to air pollution from auto exhaust but had lower / normal risk of heart disease.
436	
437	Ortiz definitely never cites such data points in his reply.
438	(E) Ortiz is saying, "are you sure that just because living on busy streets and having higher risk of heart disease are correlated that one causes the other? Couldn't an alternate explanation be that people who live on busy streets have certain lifestyle factors, like smoking or drinking, that are the real reason their heart disease risk is higher?"

489	STIMULUS: Columnist: If you received an unsigned letter, you would likely have some doubts about the truth of its contents. 800	This feels like how we often analyze the Curious Fact / Explanation arguments we see in Strengthen and Weaken. Our author says, "Hey you can't be so sure of that explanation. 941	(E) [84% | Correct] It is the primary evidence from which the argument’s conclusion is drawn.
942	--- BREAKDOWN
943	## Conclusion (seems to be)
944	The economy seems to be heading out of recession.
945	## Intermediate Conclusion (indicating that)
946	Consumers expect economic growth in the near future.

949	## Evaluate
950	It's a little tricky to figure out the intended argument relationships, unless we know our keywords or unless we know how Role and Main Conclusion typically work.
951	  Most arguments on Role and Main Conclusion give us the main conclusion **earlier** that its support. These are the two main forms:
952	  1. Conclusion.            1. Attribution (*some say*)
953	2. After all, support.    2. (*however*) Conclusion.
954	                                    3. After all, support.
955	  This argument has the structure on the left, only it has three claims.
956	   *Claim 1. (Conclusion)*
957	      *After all, Claim 2 (Premise)*
958	          *and thus Claim 3 (Intermediate Conclusion).*
959	  What keywords would assist us in decoding this structure?
960	  *Seems to be* = opinion
961	*Recent figures =* fact
962	*indicating that* = inference drawn from previous claim
963	  When it comes to the 2nd sentence, we know that the second half (prefaced by ****indicating that****) is being supported by the first half (prefaced by ****recent figures show****).
964	  If we're doing Role or Main Conclusion and the first sentence sounds like the Author's Opinion, then 99% of the time that claim will be the main conclusion. Try to hear the rest of the paragraph as though it's meant to be bundled together to collectively support that first claim.

966	They're asking us about claim 2, which was definitely Support.
967	  If we want to get more nuanced, we can say that it directly supports claim 3 (an intermediate conclusion) and indirectly supports claim 1 (the main conclusion.

1369	STEM: The argument does which one of the following?
1370	(A) [93% | Correct] tries to show that a set of principles is limited in a specific way by using an analogy to a similar principle that is limited in a similar way
1371	(B) [1% | Bad Premise Match] infers a claim by arguing that the truth of that claim would best explain observed facts
1372	(C) [0% | Bad Premise Match] presents numerous experimental results as evidence for a general principle
1373	(D) [2% | Bad Conclusion + Premise Match] attempts to demonstrate that an explanation of a phenomenon is flawed by showing that it fails to explain a particular instance of that phenomenon
1374	(E) [4% | Bad Conclusion + Premise Match] applies an empirical generalization to reach a conclusion about a particular case
1375	--- BREAKDOWN
1376	## Conclusion
1377	It's not a violation of free-market principles if a government on the verge of a economic collapse needs to limit the extent to which foreign investors and lenders can pull out their money.
1378	## Evidence
1379	It's not a violation of free-speech principles to say that you can't yell "Fire!" in a crowded movie theater, because that could lead to a harmful stampede. Similarly, letting foreign investors and lenders stampede to get their money out of an imperiled economy could do harm.
1380	## Evaluation
1381	Our job is just to characterize the type of evidence or type of move the author made to support her conclusion. The most common type of correct answer on Method is questions is that the author used an analogy to support her argument, and that seems to be what's happening here. To make a point about governments limiting free-market activities, the author used an analogy about a case we already accept in which governments limit free-speech activities. In both cases, the point of doing so is the avoidance of harm.
1382	## Goal
1383	Look for something saying that the author used an analogy to show that placing some limits on an activity in order to avoid harm can be a reasonable position and not a violation of the underlying principle of freedom.

1385	(A) Yes, the author tries to show that free-market principles are limited in a certain way (can't rapidly withdraw money when system is on verge of collapse) by using the analogy that free-speech principles are limited (can't yell "Fire!" in crowded theater). They are limited in similar ways in that both limitations are an attempt to avoid potential harm.
1386	(B) Does the author's conclusion infer a claim? Sure. That's so generic that it would apply to all conclusions. Does the evidence say, "After all, if this claim were true, it would offer the best explanation for something we've observed?" Heavens no. There are no observed facts in this argument. It's all hypothetical / theoretical stuff.
1387	(C) Does the author provide numerous experimental results? Not even close. Nothing here is empirical. It's all just theoretical / hypothetical ideas.
1388	(D) Does the author's conclusion try to show that someone's explanation for a phenomenon is flawed? Not at all. An explanation for a phenomenon means that something happened and someone has proposed a causal story for why it happened. Nothing in this argument "happened". There are no past tense, real world facts. It's just an abstract debate about hypothetical's and political philosophy. We don't need to keep reading, but the premise also doesn't match, since there are no particular instances of a government limiting foreigners from withdrawing their money mentioned in the evidence.
1389	(E) Does the author reach a conclusion about a particular case? No, she reaches a conclusion about a whole category of hypothetical cases --- "whenever governments are considering limiting the rate at which foreigners can withdraw money from an imperiled economy".

1396	(B) [12% | Contradicted] asserting that the case of clairvoyance is one of knowledge even though we do not really believe in clairvoyance as a reliable process
1397	(C) [1% | Contradicted] arguing against the assumption that clairvoyance is unreliable
1398	(D) [1% | Out of Scope: personal choice] explaining that the definition of knowledge is a matter of personal choice

1400	--- BREAKDOWN
1401	## Definition
1402	True belief formed → Knowledge by reliable process
1403	## Objection
1404	If someone was using the process of clairvoyance to reliably come to true beliefs, we wouldn't call that knowledge. (this is trying to show the definition is wrong, by pointing to a counterexample that matches the trigger but not the outcome)
1405	## Defense
1406	You're right, we wouldn't call clairvoyance knowledge under my definition, but not because the definition is wrong. 1486	(C) [1% | Bad Premise Match] objecting that the statistics on which the legislator is basing his conclusion are drawn from a time period that is too short to yield a meaningful data sample
