1520	STIMULUS: It would not be surprising to discover that the trade routes between China and the West were opened many centuries, even millennia, earlier than 200 B.C., contrary to what is currently believed. After all, what made the Great Silk Road so attractive as a trade route linking China and the West—level terrain, easily traversable mountain passes, and desert oases—would also have made it an attractive route for the original emigrants to China from Africa and the Middle East, and this early migration began at least one million years ago.
1521	STEM: That a migration from Africa and the Middle East to China occurred at least one million years ago figures in the above reasoning in which one of the following ways?
1522	(A) [6% | Too Strong: “conclusive”] It is cited as conclusive evidence for the claim that trade links between China and the Middle East were established long before 200 B.C.
1523	(B) [9% | Not Subsidiary Conclusion] It is an intermediate conclusion made plausible by the description of the terrain along which the migration supposedly took place.
1524	(C) [83% | Correct] It is offered as evidence in support of the claim that trade routes between China and the West could easily have been established much earlier than is currently believed.
1525	(D) [1% | Unmentioned / Out of Scope: “preceded”] It is offered as evidence against the claim that trade routes between China and Africa preceded those eventually established between China and the Middle East.
1526	(E) [2% | Not The Conclusion] It is the main conclusion that the argument attempts to establish about intercourse between China and the West.
1527	--- BREAKDOWN
1528	## Conclusion (opinion = not surprising)
1529	It would not be surprising if the trade routes between China and the West were open way earlier than is currently thought (~2200 years ago).
1530	## Evidence (after all, and)
1531	These trade routes were attractive because of their easily traversable terrain. This trait would have likely made it an attractive option for migrations that began one million years ago.
1532	## Evaluation
1533	The conclusion is the first sentence, as is very common on Role and Main Conclusion questions. The "after all" tells us that what follows will be Support for that first sentence. The final sentence makes two claims, joined by an "and". 99% of the time "and" is connecting to supporting ideas.
1534	## Goal
1535	The claim they're asking about is one of our two premises, for the conclusion that is found in the first sentence.
1536	--- CHOICE NOTES
1537	(A) Too strong to call this conclusive evidence, when the conclusion’s wording is only as strong as “it wouldn’t be surprising”.
1538	Conclusive evidence means that it 100% proves the conclusion. If you have conclusive evidence, then the conclusion follows logically / is properly drawn / is properly inferred.
1539	(B) It is not a subsidiary conclusion, because no support is provided for it. Why should we believe that a migration from Africa / Middle East to China occurred a million years ago? The argument doesn’t provide any support for that idea, so it’s impossible to regard that claim as a conclusion.
1540	(C) It’s a premise (i.e. evidence/support) and the conclusion is the first sentence.
1541	This is a bit of a weird meaning match, but when we say that something "would not be surprising", we mean "it would very much match expectations", and thus it's a pretty fair match for saying "it something that could easily be true".
1542	Suppose we know that Tina attends law school at Yale.
1543	We might say, "It would not be surprising if Tina had a really high LSAT score. It would match my expectations if I found out that Tina had a high LSAT score. It could easily be true that Tina had a high LSAT score."
1544	(D) The author never tries to differentiate a China ↔ Africa route from a China ↔ Middle East one. When our author brings up Africa and the Middle East, they are lumped together into the same timeframe.
1545	(E) The conclusion is the first sentence, not this claim. Any time two claims are joined by “and”, neither one of them is going to be the conclusion.

I read all 80 candidates in full and checked every choice note against the printed choice text. Both shortlists had more than 10 good candidates, so I didn't look beyond them. I edited no other file and made no database writes.

Six golden items were in the shortlists and still hold up: PT111-S4-Q4, PT143-S1-Q6 and PT106-S2-Q26 (Paradox), and PT133-S1-Q17, PT144-S2-Q8 and PT147-S4-Q12 (Method). I left them out of the tables, so all 40 picks are new.

**Paradox (20 picks, difficulty 1 to 5)**
- **Coverage:** difference-makers, four math paradoxes, five plan-didn't-work or backfire items, four EXCEPT items, "explain all the data" (PT121-S4-Q19), and one key that works by undercutting the first fact (PT120-S1-Q6).
- **Top scores (12/12):** PT148-S3-Q11, PT139-S1-Q15 and PT152-S4-Q23.
- **What the best ones do:**
  - They name the expectation and the kind of fix before reading the choices.
  - They give two or three shapes the answer could take, then sort every choice into one.
  - They use one reusable test, such as "what changed?" or "is this a timeless rule?".
  - They use the background fact to kill the popular trap.
- **Moved in from the Method shortlist:** PT148-S1-Q12. Its stem ("explain the failure of the strategy") makes it a Paradox.

**Method (20 picks, difficulty 1 to 5)**
- **Coverage:** analogy, breaking an analogy, counterexample, absurd implications, alternate explanation, effect without the cause, defending a definition, and reinterpreting a key phrase.
- **Plans gap:** three picks cover Method on Plans/Recommendations, which the golden set had no model for: PT102-S4-Q19, PT108-S3-Q13 and PT118-S1-Q7.
- **Top scores (12/12):** PT140-S1-Q9 and PT139-S1-Q23.
- **What the best ones do:**
  - They name the move from a fixed menu of eight.
  - They split every answer into a conclusion half and an evidence half and check both.
  - They apply the rule that the second speaker almost never denies the first speaker's evidence.
  - They teach strength words ("unwarranted" is not "false"; "inconsistent" is almost never right).
  - They rewrite a wrong answer into the version that would have been correct.

**Fidelity problems** (the full list is in the file)
- **Misread choice:** the PT128-S2-Q19 B note argues as if the choice said most crimes *are* reported. The choice says they are not.
- **Disqualified for tone:** PT149-S1-Q10 ("hot garbage"), PT148-S1-Q20 ("so, so dumb") and PT138-S3-Q20 ("This correct answer is miserable").
- **Logic Games conflict:** PT137-S4-Q3 builds its main analogy on Logic Games practice, which breaks the no-LG rule for student copy.
- **Small slips:**
  - PT143-S1-Q19 switches from OKC to "Austin" mid-example.
  - PT135-S4-Q16's Goal says "the critic" where it means the economist.
  - PT106-S1-Q7 says "mental disorders" where the stimulus says neurological.
  - PT151-S4-Q21 says "has been attributed" where it means "has not been".
- **Damaged transcription:** lost spaces or flattened diagrams in PT126-S4-Q5, PT104-S4-Q8, PT106-S1-Q17 and PT105-S2-Q21.
- **Different question type:** nine shortlisted candidates had stems of another type. Eight were skipped:
  - Necessary Assumption: PT103-S2-Q25, PT151-S2-Q5 and PT115-S2-Q7.
  - Role: PT101-S2-Q10 and PT131-S1-Q14.
  - PT122-S4-Q8 (weaken a method's reliability) and PT111-S3-Q23 (the conclusion a reply leads to).
  - PT143-S1-Q21 (a Strengthen stem in the Paradox list).

  The ninth, PT148-S1-Q12, moved to Paradox as described above. I also skipped PT106-S3-Q13 as a borderline case: "resolve a dispute in favor of one party" is closer to Strengthen.

Scores are one careful read each, so treat 11 and 12 as a tie.
